Julius Caesar

William Shakespeare — EMOTHE0240_JuliusCaesar

Act I

[1.1]

Enter Flavius, Murellus, and certain commoners over the stage.

FLAVIUS

1Hence! Home, you idle creatures! Get you home!
2Is this a holiday? What, know you not,
3Being mechanical, you ought not walk
4Upon a laboring day, without the sign
5Of your profession? Speak, what trade art thou?

CARPENTER

Why, sir, a carpenter.

MURELLUS

6Where is thy leather apron and thy rule?
7What dost thou with thy best apparel on?
8You, sir, what trade are you?

COBBLER

Truly, sir, in respect of a fine workman, I am but as you would say, a cobbler.

MURELLUS

But what trade art thou? Answer me directly.

COBBLER

A trade, sir, that I hope I may use, with a safe conscience, which is indeed sir, a mender of bad soles.

FLAVIUS

What trade, thou knave? Thou naughty knave, what trade?

COBBLER

Nay, I beseech you, sir, be not out with me. Yet if you be out, sir, I can mend you.

MURELLUS

What mean'st thou by that? Mend me, thou saucy fellow?

COBBLER

Why, sir, cobble you.

FLAVIUS

Thou art a cobbler, art thou?

COBBLER

Truly sir, all that I live by is with the awl. I meddle with no tradesman's matters, nor women's matters; but withal I am indeed, sir, a surgeon to old shoes: when they are in great danger, I recover them. As proper men as ever trod upon neat's leather, have gone upon my handiwork.

FLAVIUS

9But wherefore art not in thy shop today?
10Why dost thou lead these men about the streets?

COBBLER

Truly sir, to wear out their shoes, to get myself into more work. But indeed, sir, we make holiday to see Caesar, and to rejoice in his triumph.

MURELLUS

11Wherefore rejoice? What conquest brings he home?
12What tributaries follow him to Rome,
13To grace in captive bonds his chariot wheels?
14You blocks! You stones! You worse than senseless things!
15O you hard hearts! You cruel men of Rome!
16Knew you not Pompey many a time and oft?
17Have you climbed up to walls and battlements,
18To towers and windows, yea, to chimney tops,
19Your infants in your arms, and there have sat
20The livelong day, with patient expectation,
21To see great Pompey pass the streets of Rome?
22And when you saw his chariot but appear,
23Have you not made an universal shout,
24That Tiber trembled underneath her banks
25To hear the replication of your sounds,
26Made in her concave shores?
27And do you now put on your best attire?
28And do you now cull out a holiday?
29And do you now strew flowers in his way
30That comes in triumph over Pompey's blood?
31Begone!
32Run to your houses! Fall upon your knees!
33Pray to the gods to intermit the plague
34That needs must light on this ingratitude!

FLAVIUS

35Go, go, good countrymen, and for this fault
36Assemble all the poor men of your sort;
37Draw them to Tiber banks, and weep your tears
38Into the channel, till the lowest stream
39Do kiss the most exalted shores of all.

Exeunt all the Commoners.

40See whe'er their basest mettle be not moved:
41They vanish tongue-tied in their guiltiness.
42Go you down that way towards the Capitol;
43This way will I. Disrobe the images,
44If you do find them decked with ceremonies.

MURELLUS

45May we do so?
46You know it is the Feast of Lupercal.

FLAVIUS

47It is no matter. Let no images
48Be hung with Caesar's trophies. I'll about,
49And drive away the vulgar from the streets;
50So do you too, where you perceive them thick.
51These growing feathers, plucked from Caesar's wing,
52Will make him fly an ordinary pitch,
53Who else would soar above the view of men,
54And keep us all in servile fearfulness.

Exeunt

[1.2]

Enter Caesar, Antony for the course, Calpurnia, Portia, Decius, Cicero, Brutus, Cassius, Casca, a Soothsayer; after them Murellus and Flavius.

CAESAR

55Calpurnia.

CASCA

Calpurnia. Peace ho! Caesar speaks.

CAESAR

Calpurnia. Peace ho! Caesar speaks. Calpurnia!

CALPURNIA

56Here, my Lord.

CAESAR

57Stand you directly in Antonio's way
58When he doth run his course. Antonio!

ANTONY

59Caesar, my Lord?

CAESAR

60Forget not in your speed, Antonio,
61To touch Calpurnia, for our elders say,
62The barren touchèd in this holy chase,
63Shake off their sterile curse.

ANTONY

Shake off their sterile curse. I shall remember.
64When Caesar says, "Do this," it is performed.

CAESAR

65Set on, and leave no ceremony out.

[Sennet]

SOOTHSAYER

66Caesar!

CAESAR

67Ha? Who calls?

CASCA

68Bid every noise be still! Peace yet again!

CAESAR

69Who is it in the press that calls on me?
70I hear a tongue shriller than all the music
71Cry "Caesar." Speak! Caesar is turned to hear.

SOOTHSAYER

72Beware the ides of March.

CAESAR

Beware the ides of March. What man is that?

BRUTUS

73A soothsayer bids you beware the ides of March.

CAESAR

74Set him before me. Let me see his face.

CASSIUS

75Fellow, come from the throng! Look upon Caesar.

CAESAR

76What say'st thou to me now? Speak once again.

SOOTHSAYER

77Beware the ides of March.

CAESAR

78He is a dreamer. Let us leave him. Pass.

Sennet.

Exeunt [all but Brutus and Cassius].

CASSIUS

79Will you go see the order of the course?

BRUTUS

80Not I.

CASSIUS

81I pray you, do.

BRUTUS

82I am not gamesome. I do lack some part
83Of that quick spirit that is in Antony.
84Let me not hinder, Cassius, your desires.
85I'll leave you.

CASSIUS

86Brutus, I do observe you now of late.
87I have not from your eyes that gentleness
88And show of love as I was wont to have.
89You bear too stubborn and too strange a hand
90Over your friend that loves you.

BRUTUS

Over your friend that loves you. Cassius,
91Be not deceived. If I have veiled my look,
92I turn the trouble of my countenance
93Merely upon myself. Vexed I am
94Of late with passions of some difference,
95Conceptions only proper to myself,
96Which give some soil, perhaps, to my behaviors;
97But let not therefore my good friends be grieved,
98Among which number, Cassius, be you one,
99Nor construe any further my neglect
100Than that poor Brutus with himself at war,
101Forgets the shows of love to other men.

CASSIUS

102Then Brutus, I have much mistook your passion,
103By means whereof, this breast of mine hath buried
104Thoughts of great value, worthy cogitations.
105Tell me, good Brutus, can you see your face?

BRUTUS

106No, Cassius, for the eye sees not itself
107But by reflection, by some other things.

CASSIUS

108'Tis just.
109And it is very much lamented, Brutus,
110That you have no such mirrors as will turn
111Your hidden worthiness into your eye,
112That you might see your shadow. I have heard,
113Where many of the best respect in Rome,
114Except immortal Caesar, speaking of Brutus
115And groaning underneath this age's yoke,
116Have wished that noble Brutus had his eyes.

BRUTUS

117Into what dangers, would you lead me, Cassius,
118That you would have me seek into myself
119For that which is not in me?

CASSIUS

120Therefore, good Brutus, be prepared to hear;
121And since you know you cannot see yourself
122So well as by reflection, I, your glass,
123Will modestly discover to yourself
124That of yourself which you yet know not of.
125And be not jealous on me, gentle Brutus.
126Were I a common laughter, or did use
127To stale with ordinary oaths my love
128To every new protester; if you know
129That I do fawn on men and hug them hard
130And after scandal them; or if you know
131That I profess myself in banqueting
132To all the rout, then hold me dangerous.

Flourish, and shout.

BRUTUS

133What means this shouting? I do fear the people
134Choose Caesar for their king.

CASSIUS

Choose Caesar for their king. Ay, do you fear it?
135Then must I think you would not have it so.

BRUTUS

136I would not, Cassius, yet I love him well.
137But wherefore do you hold me here so long?
138What is it that you would impart to me?
139If it be ought toward the general good,
140Set honor in one eye and death i'th'other,
141And I will look on both indifferently.
142For let the gods so speed me, as I love
143The name of honor more than I fear death.

CASSIUS

144I know that virtue to be in you, Brutus,
145As well as I do know your outward favor.
146Well, honor is the subject of my story.
147I cannot tell what you and other men
148Think of this life, but for my single self,
149I had as lief not be, as live to be
150In awe of such a thing as I myself.
151I was born free as Caesar, so were you;
152We both have fed as well; and we can both
153Endure the winter's cold, as well as he.
154For once, upon a raw and gusty day,
155The troubled Tiber, chafing with her shores,
156Caesar said to me, "Dar'st thou, Cassius, now
157Leap in with me into this angry flood
158And swim to yonder point?" Upon the word,
159Accoutred as I was, I plungèd in,
160And bade him follow. So indeed he did.
161The torrent roared, and we did buffet it
162With lusty sinews, throwing it aside,
163And stemming it with hearts of controversy.
164But ere we could arrive the point proposed,
165Caesar cried, "Help me, Cassius, or I sink."
166I, as Aeneas, our great ancestor,
167Did from the flames of Troy upon his shoulder
168The old Anchises bear, so, from the waves of Tiber
169Did I the tirèd Caesar. And this man
170Is now become a god, and Cassius is
171A wretched creature and must bend his body
172If Caesar carelessly but nod on him.
173He had a fever when he was in Spain,
174And when the fit was on him, I did mark
175How he did shake. 'Tis true, this god did shake.
176His coward lips did from their color fly,
177And that same eye, whose bend doth awe the world,
178Did lose his luster. I did hear him groan.
179Ay, and that tongue of his, that bade the Romans
180Mark him and write his speeches in their books,
181"Alas," it cried, "Give me some drink, Titinius,"
182As a sick girl. Ye gods, it doth amaze me
183A man of such a feeble temper should
184So get the start of the majestic world
185And bear the palm alone.

Shout. Flourish.

BRUTUS

186Another general shout?
187I do believe that these applauses are
188For some new honors that are heaped on Caesar.

CASSIUS

189Why man, he doth bestride the narrow world
190Like a colossus, and we petty men
191Walk under his huge legs, and peep about
192To find ourselves dishonorable graves.
193Men at some time are masters of their fates.
194The fault, dear Brutus, is not in our stars,
195But in ourselves that we are underlings.
196"Brutus" and "Caesar." What should be in that "Caesar"?
197Why should that name be sounded more than yours?
198Write them together: yours is as fair a name.
199Sound them: it doth become the mouth as well.
200Weigh them: it is as heavy. Conjure with 'em:
201"Brutus" will start a spirit as soon as "Caesar."
202Now in the names of all the gods at once,
203Upon what meat doth this our Caesar feed
204That he is grown so great? Age, thou art shamed!
205Rome, thou hast lost the breed of noble bloods!
206When went there by an age since the great flood,
207But it was famed with more than with one man?
208When could they say, till now, that talked of Rome,
209That her wide walks encompassed but one man?
210Now is it Rome indeed, and room enough
211When there is in it but one only man.
212Oh, you and I have heard our fathers say
213There was a Brutus once that would have brooked
214Th'eternal devil to keep his state in Rome
215As easily as a king.

BRUTUS

216That you do love me, I am nothing jealous.
217What you would work me to, I have some aim.
218How I have thought of this and of these times
219I shall recount hereafter. For this present,
220I would not, so with love I might entreat you,
221Be any further moved. What you have said,
222I will consider; what you have to say,
223I will with patience hear, and find a time
224Both meet to hear and answer such high things.
225Till then, my noble friend, chew upon this:
226Brutus had rather be a villager
227Than to repute himself a son of Rome
228Under these hard conditions as this time
229Is like to lay upon us.

CASSIUS

230I am glad that my weak words
231Have struck but thus much show of fire from Brutus.

Enter Caesar and his train.

BRUTUS

232The games are done, and Caesar is returning.

CASSIUS

233As they pass by, pluck Casca by the sleeve,
234And he will, after his sour fashion, tell you
235What hath proceeded worthy note today.

BRUTUS

236I will do so. But look you, Cassius,
237The angry spot doth glow on Caesar's brow,
238And all the rest look like a chidden train:
239Calpurnia's cheek is pale, and Cicero
240Looks with such ferret and such fiery eyes
241As we have seen him in the Capitol,
242Being crossed in conference by some senators.

CASSIUS

243Casca will tell us what the matter is.

CAESAR

244Antonio!

ANTONY

245Caesar?

CAESAR

246Let me have men about me that are fat,
247Sleek-headed men, and such as sleep o'nights.
248Yond Cassius has a lean and hungry look.
249He thinks too much. Such men are dangerous.

ANTONY

250Fear him not Caesar, he's not dangerous,
251He is a noble Roman, and well given.

CAESAR

252Would he were fatter! But I fear him not.
253Yet if my name were liable to fear,
254I do not know the man I should avoid
255So soon as that spare Cassius. He reads much,
256He is a great observer, and he looks
257Quite through the deeds of men. He loves no plays,
258As thou dost, Antony; he hears no music;
259Seldom he smiles, and smiles in such a sort
260As if he mocked himself and scorned his spirit
261That could be moved to smile at anything.
262Such men as he be never at heart's ease
263Whiles they behold a greater than themselves,
264And therefore are they very dangerous.
265I rather tell thee what is to be feared
266Than what I fear, for always I am Caesar.
267Come on my right hand, for this ear is deaf,
268And tell me truly, what thou think'st of him.

Sennet.

Exeunt Caesar and [all] his train [but Casca].

CASCA

You pulled me by the cloak. Would you speak with me?

BRUTUS

269Ay, Casca. Tell us what hath chanced today,
270That Caesar looks so sad.

CASCA

Why, you were with him, were you not?

BRUTUS

271I should not then ask Casca what had chanced.

CASCA

Why, there was a crown offered him; and being offered him, he put it by with the back of his hand, thus, and then the people fell a-shouting.

BRUTUS

What was the second noise for?

CASCA

Why, for that too.

CASSIUS

272They shouted thrice. What was the last cry for?

CASCA

Why, for that too.

BRUTUS

Was the crown offered him thrice?

CASCA

Ay, marry, was't, and he put it by thrice, every time gentler than other; and at every putting by, mine honest neighbors shouted.

CASSIUS

273Who offered him the crown?

CASCA

Why, Antony.

BRUTUS

274Tell us the manner of it, gentle Casca.

CASCA

I can as well be hanged as tell the manner of it. It was mere foolery. I did not mark it. I saw Mark Antony offered him a crown; yet 'twas not a crown, neither; 'twas one of these coronets. And as I told you, he put it by once; but for all that, to my thinking, he would fain have had it. Then he offered it to him again; then he put it by again. But to my thinking, he was very loath to lay his fingers off it. And then he offered it the third time; he put it the third time by. And still as he refused it, the rabblement hooted, and clapped their chapped hands, and threw up their sweaty nightcaps, and uttered such a deal of stinking breath, because Caesar refused the crown, that it had almost choked Caesar, for he swooned, and fell down at it. And for mine own part, I durst not laugh, for fear of opening my lips and receiving the bad air.

CASSIUS

275But soft, I pray you: what, did Caesar swoon?

CASCA

He fell down in the marketplace and foamed at mouth, and was speechless.

BRUTUS

276'Tis very like. He hath the falling sickness.

CASSIUS

277No, Caesar hath it not, but you and I,
278And honest Casca: we have the falling sickness.

CASCA

I know not what you mean by that, but I am sure Caesar fell down. If the tag-rag people did not clap him, and hiss him, according as he pleased, and displeased them, as they use to do the players in the theater, I am no true man.

BRUTUS

279What said he, when he came unto himself?

CASCA

Marry, before he fell down, when he perceived the common herd was glad he refused the crown, he plucked me ope his doublet, and offered them his throat to cut. And I had been a man of any occupation, if I would not have taken him at a word, I would I might go to hell among the rogues, and so he fell. When he came to himself again, he said, if he had done or said anything amiss, he desired their worships to think it was his infirmity. Three or four wenches where I stood, cried, "Alas, good soul," and forgave him with all their hearts. But there's no heed to be taken of them. If Caesar had stabbed their mothers, they would have done no less.

BRUTUS

280And after that, he came thus sad away.

CASCA

Ay.

CASSIUS

Did Cicero say anything?

CASCA

Ay, he spoke Greek.

CASSIUS

To what effect?

CASCA

Nay, and I tell you that, I'll ne'er look you i'th'face again. But those that understood him, smiled at one another and shook their heads. But for mine own part, it was Greek to me. I could tell you more news too: Murellus and Flavius, for pulling scarves off Caesar's images, are put to silence. Fare you well. There was more foolery yet, if I could remember it.

CASSIUS

Will you sup with me tonight, Casca?

CASCA

No, I am promised forth.

CASSIUS

Will you dine with me tomorrow?

CASCA

Ay, if I be alive, and your mind hold, and your dinner worth the eating.

CASSIUS

Good, I will expect you.

CASCA

Do so. Farewell both.

Exit.

BRUTUS

281What a blunt fellow is this grown to be?
282He was quick mettle when he went to school.

CASSIUS

283So is he now, in execution
284Of any bold, or noble enterprise,
285However he puts on this tardy form.
286This rudeness is a sauce to his good wit,
287Which gives men stomach to digest his words
288With better appetite.

BRUTUS

With better appetite. And so it is.
289For this time I will leave you:
290Tomorrow, if you please to speak with me,
291I will come home to you, or if you will,
292Come home to me, and I will wait for you.

CASSIUS

293I will do so. Till then, think of the world.

Exit Brutus.

294Well, Brutus, thou art noble, yet I see
295Thy honorable mettle may be wrought
296From that it is disposed. Therefore it is meet
297That noble minds keep ever with their likes,
298For who so firm that cannot be seduced?
299Caesar doth bear me hard, but he loves Brutus.
300If I were Brutus now, and he were Cassius,
301He should not humor me. I will this night
302In several hands in at his windows throw,
303As if they came from several citizens,
304Writings, all tending to the great opinion
305That Rome holds of his name, wherein obscurely
306Caesar's ambition shall be glancèd at.
307And after this, let Caesar seat him sure,
308For we will shake him, or worse days endure.

Exit.

[1.3]

Thunder and lightning. Enter Casca and Cicero.

CICERO

309Good even, Casca. Brought you Caesar home?
310Why are you breathless, and why stare you so?

CASCA

311Are not you moved, when all the sway of earth
312Shakes like a thing unfirm? O Cicero,
313I have seen tempests when the scolding winds
314Have rived the knotty oaks, and I have seen
315Th'ambitious ocean swell, and rage, and foam,
316To be exalted with the threatening clouds,
317But never till tonight, never till now,
318Did I go through a tempest dropping fire.
319Either there is a civil strife in heaven,
320Or else the world, too saucy with the gods,
321Incenses them to send destruction.

CICERO

322Why, saw you any thing more wonderful?

CASCA

323A common slave, you know him well by sight,
324Held up his left hand, which did flame and burn
325Like twenty torches joined; and yet his hand,
326Not sensible of fire, remained unscorched.
327Besides—I ha'not since put up my sword—
328Against the Capitol I met a lion,
329Who glazed upon me and went surly by,
330Without annoying me. And there were drawn
331Upon a heap a hundred ghastly women,
332Transformèd with their fear, who swore they saw
333Men all in fire walk up and down the streets.
334And yesterday, the bird of night did sit
335Even at noonday upon the marketplace,
336Hooting and shrieking. When these prodigies
337Do so conjointly meet, let not men say,
338"These are their reasons, they are natural,"
339For I believe they are portentous things
340Unto the climate that they point upon.

CICERO

341Indeed, it is a strange disposèd time.
342But men may construe things after their fashion
343Clean from the purpose of the things themselves.
344Comes Caesar to the Capitol tomorrow?

CASCA

345He doth, for he did bid Antonio
346Send word to you he would be there tomorrow.

CICERO

347Good night then, Casca. This disturbèd sky
348Is not to walk in.

CASCA

Is not to walk in. Farewell Cicero.

Exit Cicero.

Enter Cassius.

CASSIUS

349Who's there?

CASCA

Who's there? A Roman.

CASSIUS

Who's there? A Roman. Casca, by your voice.

CASCA

350Your ear is good. Cassius, what night is this?

CASSIUS

351A very pleasing night to honest men.

CASCA

352Who ever knew the heavens menace so?

CASSIUS

353Those that have known the earth so full of faults.
354For my part, I have walked about the streets,
355Submitting me unto the perilous night,
356And thus unbracèd, Casca, as you see,
357Have bared my bosom to the thunder-stone,
358And when the cross blue lightning seemed to open
359The breast of heaven, I did present myself
360Even in the aim and very flash of it.

CASCA

361But wherefore did you so much tempt the heavens?
362It is the part of men to fear and tremble,
363When the most mighty gods by tokens send
364Such dreadful heralds to astonish us.

CASSIUS

365You are dull, Casca, And those sparks of life
366That should be in a Roman you do want,
367Or else you use not. You look pale, and gaze,
368And put on fear, and cast yourself in wonder,
369To see the strange impatience of the heavens.
370But if you would consider the true cause
371Why all these fires, why all these gliding ghosts,
372Why birds and beasts from quality and kind,
373Why old men, fools, and children calculate,
374Why all these things change from their ordinance,
375Their natures and preformèd faculties
376To monstrous quality—why, you shall find
377That heaven hath infused them with these spirits
378To make them instruments of fear and warning
379Unto some monstrous state.
380Now could I, Casca, name to thee a man,
381Most like this dreadful night,
382That thunders, lightens, opens graves, and roars,
383As doth the lion in the Capitol,
384A man no mightier than thyself or me
385In personal action, yet prodigious grown
386And fearful, as these strange eruptions are.

CASCA

387'Tis Caesar that you mean. Is it not, Cassius?

CASSIUS

388Let it be who it is, for Romans now
389Have thews and limbs like to their ancestors.
390But woe the while, our fathers' minds are dead,
391And we are governed with our mothers' spirits.
392Our yoke and sufferance show us womanish.

CASCA

393Indeed, they say, the senators tomorrow
394Mean to establish Caesar as a king,
395And he shall wear his crown by sea and land
396In every place, save here in Italy.

CASSIUS

397I know where I will wear this dagger then:
398Cassius from bondage will deliver Cassius.
399Therein, ye gods, you make the weak most strong,
400Therein, ye gods, you tyrants do defeat.
401Nor stony tower, nor walls of beaten brass,
402Nor airless dungeon, nor strong links of iron
403Can be retentive to the strength of spirit,
404But life, being weary of these worldly bars,
405Never lacks power to dismiss itself.
406If I know this, know all the world besides,
407That part of tyranny that I do bear
408Thunder still. Thunder still. I can shake off at pleasure.

CASCA

Thunder still. Thunder still. I can shake off at pleasure. So can I:

Thunder still.

409So every bondman in his own hand bears
410The power to cancel his captivity.

CASSIUS

411And why should Caesar be a tyrant then?
412Poor man, I know he would not be a wolf,
413But that he sees the Romans are but sheep;
414He were no lion, were not Romans hinds.
415Those that with haste will make a mighty fire
416Begin it with weak straws. What trash is Rome,
417What rubbish and what offal, when it serves
418For the base matter to illuminate
419So vile a thing as Caesar! But, O grief,
420Where hast thou led me? I perhaps speak this
421Before a willing bondman; then I know
422My answer must be made. But I am armed,
423And dangers are to me indifferent.

CASCA

424You speak to Casca, and to such a man
425That is no fleering tell-tale. Hold, my hand.
426Be factious for redress of all these griefs,
427And I will set this foot of mine as far
428As who goes farthest.

CASSIUS

As who goes farthest. There's a bargain made.
429Now know you, Casca, I have moved already
430Some certain of the noblest-minded Romans
431To undergo with me an enterprise
432Of honorable dangerous consequence,
433And I do know by this, they stay for me
434In Pompey's porch. For now, this fearful night,
435There is no stir or walking in the streets,
436And the complexion of the element
437In favor's like the work we have in hand:
438Most bloody, fiery, and most terrible.

Enter Cinna.

CASCA

439Stand close awhile, for here comes one in haste.

CASSIUS

440'Tis Cinna; I do know him by his gait;
441He is a friend. Cinna, where haste you so?

CINNA

442To find out you. Who's that, Metellus Cimber?

CASSIUS

443No, it is Casca, one incorporate
444To our attempts. Am I not stayed for, Cinna?

CINNA

445I am glad on't. What a fearful night is this?
446There's two or three of us have seen strange sights.

CASSIUS

447Am I not stayed for? Tell me.

CINNA

448Yes, you are. O Cassius, if you could
449But win the noble Brutus to our party—

CASSIUS

450Be you content. Good Cinna, take this paper,
451And look you lay it in the praetor's chair,
452Where Brutus may but find it. And throw this
453In at his window. Set this up with wax
454Upon old Brutus' statue. All this done,
455Repair to Pompey's porch, where you shall find us.
456Is Decius Brutus and Trebonius there?

CINNA

457All but Metellus Cimber, and he's gone
458To seek you at your house. Well, I will hie,
459And so bestow these papers as you bade me.

CASSIUS

460That done, repair to Pompey's theater.

Exit Cinna.

461Come, Casca, you and I will yet, ere day,
462See Brutus at his house. Three parts of him
463Is ours already, and the man entire
464Upon the next encounter yields him ours.

CASCA

465Oh, he sits high in all the people's hearts,
466And that which would appear offense in us,
467His countenance, like richest alchemy,
468Will change to virtue and to worthiness.

CASSIUS

469Him and his worth and our great need of him
470You have right well conceited. Let us go,
471For it is after midnight, and ere day,
472We will awake him and be sure of him.

Exeunt.

Act II

[2.1]

Enter Brutus in his orchard.

BRUTUS

473What, Lucius, ho!
474I cannot, by the progress of the stars,
475Give guess how near to day— Lucius, I say!
476I would it were my fault to sleep so soundly.
477When, Lucius, when? Awake, I say! What, Lucius!

Enter Lucius.

LUCIUS

478Called you, my lord?

BRUTUS

479Get me a taper in my study, Lucius.
480When it is lighted, come and call me here.

LUCIUS

481I will, my Lord.

Exit.

BRUTUS

482It must be by his death, and for my part,
483I know no personal cause to spurn at him,
484But for the general. He would be crowned.
485How that might change his nature, there's the question.
486It is the bright day that brings forth the adder,
487And that craves wary walking. Crown him that,
488And then I grant we put a sting in him
489That at his will he may do danger with.
490Th'abuse of greatness is when it disjoins
491Remorse from power, and to speak truth of Caesar,
492I have not known when his affections swayed
493More than his reason. But 'tis a common proof
494That lowliness is young ambition's ladder,
495Whereto the climber upward turns his face,
496But when he once attains the upmost round,
497He then unto the ladder turns his back,
498Looks in the clouds, scorning the base degrees
499By which he did ascend. So Caesar may.
500Then lest he may, prevent. And since the quarrel
501Will bear no color for the thing he is,
502Fashion it thus: that what he is, augmented,
503Would run to these and these extremities;
504And therefore think him as a serpent's egg,
505Which hatched would, as his kind, grow mischievous,
506And kill him in the shell.

Enter Lucius.

LUCIUS

507The taper burneth in your closet, sir.
508Searching the window for a flint, I found
509This paper, thus sealed up, and I am sure
510It did not lie there when I went to bed.

Gives him the letter.

BRUTUS

511Get you to bed again; it is not day.
512Is not tomorrow, boy, the first of March?

LUCIUS

513I know not, sir.

BRUTUS

514Look in the calender, and bring me word.

LUCIUS

515I will, sir.

Exit.

BRUTUS

516The exhalations, whizzing in the air,
517Give so much light that I may read by them.

Opens the letter and reads.

518"Brutus, thou sleep'st. Awake, and see thyself.
519Shall Rome, etc. Speak, strike, redress!"
520"Brutus, thou sleep'st. Awake."
521Such instigations have been often dropped
522Where I have took them up.
523"Shall Rome, etc." Thus must I piece it out:
524Shall Rome stand under one man's awe? What Rome?
525My ancestors did from the streets of Rome
526The Tarquin drive, when he was called a king.
527"Speak, strike, redress!" Am I entreated
528To speak and strike? O Rome, I make thee promise,
529If the redress will follow, thou receivest
530Thy full petition at the hand of Brutus.

Enter Lucius.

LUCIUS

531Sir, March is wasted fifteen days.

Knock within.

BRUTUS

532'Tis good. Go to the gate, somebody knocks:

[Exit Lucius.]

533Since Cassius first did whet me against Caesar,
534I have not slept.
535Between the acting of a dreadful thing
536And the first motion, all the interim is
537Like a phantasma or a hideous dream:
538The genius and the mortal instruments
539Are then in council, and the state of man,
540Like to a little kingdom, suffers then
541The nature of an insurrection.

Enter Lucius.

LUCIUS

542Sir, 'tis your brother Cassius at the door,
543Who doth desire to see you.

BRUTUS

Who doth desire to see you. Is he alone?

LUCIUS

544No, sir. There are more with him.

BRUTUS

No, sir. There are more with him. Do you know them?

LUCIUS

545No, sir. Their hats are plucked about their ears,
546And half their faces buried in their cloaks,
547That by no means I may discover them
548By any mark of favor.

BRUTUS

By any mark of favor. Let 'em enter.

[Exit Lucius.]

549They are the faction. O conspiracy,
550Sham'st thou to show thy dangerous brow by night,
551When evils are most free? O then, by day
552Where wilt thou find a cavern dark enough
553To mask thy monstrous visage? Seek none, conspiracy!
554Hide it in smiles and affability!
555For if thou put thy native semblance on,
556Not Erebus itself were dim enough
557To hide thee from prevention.

Enter the Conspirators, Cassius, Casca, Decius, Cinna, Metellus, and Trebonius.

CASSIUS

558I think we are too bold upon your rest.
559Good morrow, Brutus. Do we trouble you?

BRUTUS

560I have been up this hour, awake all night.
561Know I these men that come along with you?

CASSIUS

562Yes, every man of them, and no man here
563But honors you, and every one doth wish
564You had but that opinion of yourself
565Which every noble Roman bears of you.
566This is Trebonius.

BRUTUS

This is Trebonius. He is welcome hither.

CASSIUS

567This, Decius Brutus.

BRUTUS

This, Decius Brutus. He is welcome too.

CASSIUS

568This, Casca; this, Cinna; and this, Metellus Cimber.

BRUTUS

569They are all welcome.
570What watchful cares do interpose themselves
571Betwixt your eyes and night?

CASSIUS

572Shall I entreat a word?

They whisper.

DECIUS

573Here lies the east. Doth not the day break here?

CASCA

574No.

CINNA

575O pardon, sir, it doth; and yon gray lines
576That fret the clouds are messengers of day.

CASCA

577You shall confess that you are both deceived.
578Here, as I point my sword, the sun arises,
579Which is a great way growing on the south,
580Weighing the youthful season of the year.
581Some two months hence, up higher toward the north
582He first presents his fire, and the high east
583Stands as the Capitol, directly here.

BRUTUS

584Give me your hands all over, one by one.

CASSIUS

585And let us swear our resolution.

BRUTUS

586No, not an oath. If not the face of men,
587The sufferance of our souls, the time's abuse—
588If these be motives weak, break off betimes,
589And every man hence, to his idle bed.
590So let high-sighted tyranny range on,
591Till each man drop by lottery. But if these,
592As I am sure they do, bear fire enough
593To kindle cowards and to steel with valor
594The melting spirits of women, then, countrymen,
595What need we any spur but our own cause
596To prick us to redress? What other bond
597Than secret Romans that have spoke the word
598And will not palter? And what other oath,
599Than honesty to honesty engaged,
600That this shall be, or we will fall for it.
601Swear priests and cowards, and men cautelous,
602Old feeble carrions and such suffering souls
603That welcome wrongs. Unto bad causes swear
604Such creatures as men doubt, but do not stain
605The even virtue of our enterprise,
606Nor th'insuppressive mettle of our spirits,
607To think that or our cause or our performance
608Did need an oath, when every drop of blood
609That every Roman bears—and nobly bears—
610Is guilty of a several bastardy,
611If he do break the smallest particle
612Of any promise that hath passed from him.

CASSIUS

613But what of Cicero? Shall we sound him?
614I think he will stand very strong with us.

CASCA

615Let us not leave him out.

CINNA

Let us not leave him out. No, by no means.

METELLUS

616O let us have him, for his silver hairs
617Will purchase us a good opinion
618And buy men's voices to commend our deeds.
619It shall be said his judgment ruled our hands.
620Our youths and wildness shall no whit appear,
621But all be buried in his gravity.

BRUTUS

622Oh, name him not; let us not break with him,
623For he will never follow anything
624That other men begin.

CASSIUS

625Then leave him out.

CASCA

Then leave him out. Indeed, he is not fit.

DECIUS

626Shall no man else be touched but only Caesar?

CASSIUS

627Decius well urged. I think it is not meet
628Mark Antony, so well beloved of Caesar,
629Should outlive Caesar. We shall find of him
630A shrewd contriver. And you know, his means,
631If he improve them, may well stretch so far
632As to annoy us all: which to prevent,
633Let Antony and Caesar fall together.

BRUTUS

634Our course will seem too bloody, Caius Cassius,
635To cut the head off and then hack the limbs,
636Like wrath in death, and envy afterwards;
637For Antony is but a limb of Caesar.
638Let's be sacrificers, but not butchers, Caius.
639We all stand up against the spirit of Caesar,
640And in the spirit of men there is no blood.
641Oh, that we then could come by Caesar's spirit,
642And not dismember Caesar! But, alas,
643Caesar must bleed for it. And, gentle friends,
644Let's kill him boldly but not wrathfully;
645Let's carve him as a dish fit for the gods,
646Not hew him as a carcass fit for hounds.
647And let our hearts, as subtle masters do,
648Stir up their servants to an act of rage
649And after seem to chide 'em. This shall make
650Our purpose necessary and not envious;
651Which so appearing to the common eyes,
652We shall be called purgers, not murderers.
653And for Mark Antony, think not of him,
654For he can do no more than Caesar's arm,
655When Caesar's head is off.

CASSIUS

When Caesar's head is off. Yet I fear him,
656For in the engrafted love he bears to Caesar—

BRUTUS

657Alas, good Cassius, do not think of him.
658If he love Caesar, all that he can do
659Is to himself: take thought, and die for Caesar.
660And that were much he should, for he is given
661To sports, to wildness, and much company.

TREBONIUS

662There is no fear in him. Let him not die,
663For he will live, and laugh at this hereafter.

Clock strikes.

BRUTUS

664Peace! Count the clock.

CASSIUS

Peace! Count the clock. The clock hath stricken three.

TREBONIUS

665'Tis time to part.

CASSIUS

'Tis time to part. But it is doubtful yet
666Whether Caesar will come forth today or no.
667For he is superstitious grown of late,
668Quite from the main opinion he held once
669Of fantasy, of dreams, and ceremonies.
670It may be these apparent prodigies,
671The unaccustomed terror of this night,
672And the persuasion of his augurers
673May hold him from the Capitol today.

DECIUS

674Never fear that. If he be so resolved
675I can o'ersway him. For he loves to hear
676That unicorns may be betrayed with trees,
677And bears with glasses; elephants, with holes;
678Lions, with toils; and men, with flatterers.
679But when I tell him he hates flatterers,
680He says he does, being then most flatterèd.
681Let me work.
682For I can give his humor the true bent,
683And I will bring him to the Capitol.

CASSIUS

684Nay, we will all of us be there to fetch him.

BRUTUS

685By the eighth hour. Is that the uttermost?

CINNA

686Be that the uttermost, and fail not then.

METELLUS

687Caius Ligarius doth bear Caesar hard,
688Who rated him for speaking well of Pompey.
689I wonder none of you have thought of him.

BRUTUS

690Now, good Metellus, go along by him.
691He loves me well, and I have given him reasons.
692Send him but hither, and I'll fashion him.

CASSIUS

693The morning comes upon's. We'll leave you, Brutus.
694And friends, disperse yourselves, but all remember
695What you have said, and show yourselves true Romans.

BRUTUS

696Good gentlemen, look fresh and merrily.
697Let not our looks put on our purposes,
698But bear it as our Roman actors do,
699With untired spirits and formal constancy.
700And so good morrow to you every one.

Exeunt [all but] Brutus.

701Boy! Lucius! Fast asleep? It is no matter.
702Enjoy the honey-heavy dew of slumber.
703Thou hast no figures nor no fantasies,
704Which busy care draws in the brains of men.
705Therefore thou sleep'st so sound.

Enter Portia. Enter Portia.

PORTIA

Therefore thou sleep'st so sound. Brutus, my Lord.

BRUTUS

706Portia! What mean you? Wherefore rise you now?
707It is not for your health thus to commit
708Your weak condition to the raw cold morning.

PORTIA

709Nor for yours neither. You've ungently, Brutus,
710Stole from my bed, and yesternight at supper
711You suddenly arose and walked about,
712Musing and sighing, with your arms across,
713And when I asked you what the matter was,
714You stared upon me, with ungentle looks.
715I urged you further; then you scratched your head
716And too impatiently stamped with your foot.
717Yet I insisted; yet you answered not,
718But with an angry wafture of your hand
719Gave sign for me to leave you. So I did,
720Fearing to strengthen that impatience
721Which seemed too much enkindled, and withal
722Hoping it was but an effect of humor,
723Which sometime hath his hour with every man.
724It will not let you eat, nor talk, nor sleep;
725And could it work so much upon your shape
726As it hath much prevailed on your condition,
727I should not know you Brutus. Dear my Lord,
728Make me acquainted with your cause of grief.

BRUTUS

729I am not well in health, and that is all.

PORTIA

730Brutus is wise, and were he not in health,
731He would embrace the means to come by it.

BRUTUS

732Why so I do. Good Portia, go to bed.

PORTIA

733Is Brutus sick? And is it physical
734To walk unbracèd and suck up the humors
735Of the dank morning? What, is Brutus sick,
736And will he steal out of his wholesome bed
737To dare the vile contagion of the night,
738And tempt the rheumy and unpurgèd air
739To add unto his sickness? No, my Brutus.
740You have some sick offense within your mind,
741Which by the right and virtue of my place
742I ought to know of. And upon my knees,
743I charm you, by my once-commended beauty,
744By all your vows of love, and that great vow
745Which did incorporate and make us one,
746That you unfold to me, your self, your half,
747Why you are heavy, and what men tonight
748Have had resort to you. For here have been
749Some six or seven who did hide their faces
750Even from darkness.

BRUTUS

Even from darkness. Kneel not, gentle Portia.

PORTIA

751I should not need, if you were gentle Brutus.
752Within the bond of marriage, tell me, Brutus,
753Is it excepted, I should know no secrets
754That appertain to you? Am I your self,
755But as it were in sort, or limitation,
756To keep with you at meals, comfort your bed,
757And talk to you sometimes? Dwell I but in the suburbs
758Of your good pleasure? If it be no more,
759Portia is Brutus' harlot, not his wife.

BRUTUS

760You are my true and honorable wife,
761As dear to me as are the ruddy drops
762That visit my sad heart.

PORTIA

763If this were true, then should I know this secret.
764I grant I am a woman, but withal
765A woman that Lord Brutus took to wife.
766I grant I am a woman, but withal
767A woman well reputed, Cato's daughter.
768Think you I am no stronger than my sex
769Being so fathered, and so husbanded?
770Tell me your counsels; I will not disclose 'em.
771I have made strong proof of my constancy,
772Giving myself a voluntary wound
773Here, in the thigh. Can I bear that with patience
774And not my husband's secrets?

BRUTUS

And not my husband's secrets? O ye gods!
775Render me worthy of this noble wife.

Knock.

776Hark, hark, one knocks! Portia go in awhile,
777And by and by thy bosom shall partake
778The secrets of my heart.
779All my engagements I will construe to thee,
780All the charactery of my sad brows.
781Leave me with haste. Ligarius. Ligarius.
782Lucius, who's that knocks.

Exit Portia. Enter Lucius and Ligarius.

LUCIUS

783Here is a sick man that would speak with you.

BRUTUS

784Caius Ligarius, that Metellus spake of.
785Boy, stand aside. Caius Ligarius, how?

LIGARIUS

786Vouchsafe good morrow from a feeble tongue.

BRUTUS

787O what a time have you chose out, brave Caius,
788To wear a kerchief. Would you were not sick!.

LIGARIUS

789I am not sick, if Brutus have in hand
790Any exploit worthy the name of honor.

BRUTUS

791Such an exploit have I in hand Ligarius,
792Had you a healthful ear to hear of it.

LIGARIUS

793By all the gods that Romans bow before,
794I here discard my sickness. Soul of Rome,
795Brave son, derived from honorable loins,
796Thou like an exorcist hast conjured up
797My mortified spirit. Now bid me run,
798And I will strive with things impossible,
799Yea, get the better of them. What's to do?

BRUTUS

800A piece of work that will make sick men whole.

LIGARIUS

801But are not some whole, that we must make sick?

BRUTUS

802That must we also. What it is my Caius,
803I shall unfold to thee as we are going
804To whom it must be done.

LIGARIUS

To whom it must be done. Set on your foot,
805And with a heart new-fired, I follow you
806To do I know not what, but it sufficeth
807That Brutus leads me on.

Thunder

BRUTUS

808Follow me, then.

Exeunt

[2.2]

Thunder and lightning. Enter Julius Caesar in his nightgown.

CAESAR

809Nor heaven nor earth have been at peace tonight.
810Thrice hath Calpurnia in her sleep cried out,
811"Help, ho, they murder Caesar!" Who's within?

Enter a servant.

SERVANT

812My Lord.

CAESAR

813Go bid the priests do present sacrifice,
814And bring me their opinions of success.

SERVANT

815I will, my lord.

Exit

Enter Calpurnia.

CALPURNIA

816What mean you, Caesar? Think you to walk forth?
817You shall not stir out of your house today.

CAESAR

818Caesar shall forth. The things that threatened me
819Ne'er looked but on my back. When they shall see
820The face of Caesar, they are vanishèd.

CALPURNIA

821Caesar, I never stood on ceremonies,
822Yet now they fright me. There is one within,
823Besides the things that we have heard and seen,
824Recounts most horrid sights seen by the watch.
825A lionness hath whelpèd in the streets,
826And graves have yawned and yielded up their dead;
827Fierce fiery warriors fight upon the clouds
828In ranks, and squadrons, and right form of war,
829Which drizzeled blood upon the Capitol.
830The noise of battle hurtled in the air:
831Horses do neigh, and dying men did groan,
832And ghosts did shriek and squeal about the streets.
833O Caesar, these things are beyond all use,
834And I do fear them.

CAESAR

And I do fear them. What can be avoided
835Whose end is purposed by the mighty gods?
836Yet Caesar shall go forth, for these predictions
837Are to the world in general as to Caesar.

CALPURNIA

838When beggars die, there are no comets seen;
839The heavens themselves blaze forth the death of princes

CAESAR

840Cowards die many times before their deaths;
841The valiant never taste of death but once.
842Of all the wonders that I yet have heard,
843It seems to me most strange that men should fear,
844Seeing that death, a necessary end,
845Will come when it will come.

Enter a servant.

CAESAR

Will come when it will come. What say the augurers?

SERVANT

846They would not have you to stir forth today.
847Plucking the entrails of an offering forth,
848They could not find a heart within the beast.

CAESAR

849The gods do this in shame of cowardice.
850Caesar should be a beast without a heart,
851If he should stay at home today for fear.
852No Caesar shall not. Danger knows full well
853That Caesar is more dangerous than he.
854We are two lions littered in one day,
855And I the elder and more terrible;
856And Caesar shall go forth.

CALPURNIA

And Caesar shall go forth. Alas, my Lord,
857Your wisdom is consumed in confidence.
858Do not go forth today. Call it my fear
859That keeps you in the house, and not your own.
860We'll send Mark Antony to the Senate House,
861And he shall say you are not well today.
862Let me upon my knee prevail in this.

CAESAR

863Mark Antony shall say I am not well,
864And for thy humor, I will stay at home.

Enter Decius.

865Here's Decius Brutus. He shall tell them so.

DECIUS

866Caesar, all hail! Good morrow, worthy Caesar,
867I come to fetch you to the Senate House.

CAESAR

868And you are come in very happy time
869To bear my greeting to the senators,
870And tell them that I will not come today—
871Cannot, is false; and that I dare not, falser:
872I will not come today. Tell them so, Decius.

CALPURNIA

873Say he is sick.

CAESAR

Say he is sick. Shall Caesar send a lie?
874Have I in conquest stretched mine arm so far
875To be afeard to tell gray-beards the truth?
876Decius, go tell them Caesar will not come.

DECIUS

877Most mighty Caesar, let me know some cause,
878Lest I be laughed at when I tell them so.

CAESAR

879The cause is in my will; I will not come.
880That is enough to satisfy the Senate.
881But for your private satisfaction,
882Because I love you, I will let you know.
883Calpurnia here, my wife, stays me at home.
884She dreamt tonight she saw my statue,
885Which like a fountain with an hundred spouts
886Did run pure blood, and many lusty Romans
887Came smiling and did bathe their hands in it.
888And these does she apply for warnings, and portents,
889And evils imminent; and on her knee
890Hath begged that I will stay at home today.

DECIUS

891This dream is all amiss interpreted:
892It was a vision fair and fortunate.
893Your statue spouting blood in many pipes,
894In which so many smiling Romans bathed,
895Signifies that from you great Rome shall suck
896Reviving blood, and that great men shall press
897For tinctures, stains, relics, and cognizance.
898This by Calpurnia's dream is signified.

CAESAR

899And this way have you well expounded it.

DECIUS

900I have, when you have heard what I can say,
901And know it now: the Senate have concluded
902To give this day a crown to mighty Caesar.
903If you shall send them word you will not come,
904Their minds may change. Besides, it were a mock
905Apt to be rendered for someone to say,
906"Break up the Senate till another time,
907When Caesar's wife shall meet with better dreams."
908If Caesar hide himself, shall they not whisper
909"Lo, Caesar is afraid"?
910Pardon me, Caesar, for my dear dear love
911To your proceeding bids me tell you this,
912And reason to my love is liable.

CAESAR

913How foolish do your fears seem now, Calpurnia?
914I am ashamèd I did yield to them.
915Give me my robe, for I will go.

Enter Brutus, Ligarius, Metellus, Casca, Trebonius, Cinna, and Publius.

916And look where Publius is come to fetch me.

PUBLIUS

917Good morrow, Caesar.

CAESAR

Good morrow, Caesar. Welcome, Publius.
918What, Brutus, are you stirred so early too?
919Good morrow, Casca. Caius Ligarius,
920Caesar was ne'er so much your enemy
921As that same ague which hath made you lean.
922What is't o'clock?

BRUTUS

What is't o'clock? Caesar, 'tis strucken eight.

CAESAR

923I thank you for your pains and courtesy.

Enter Antony.

924See, Antony, that revels long o' nights,
925Is notwithstanding up. Good morrow, Antony.

ANTONY

926So to most noble Caesar.

CAESAR

So to most noble Caesar. Bid them prepare within.
927I am to blame to be thus waited for.
928Now, Cinna. Now, Metellus. What, Trebonius,
929I have an hour's talk in store for you.
930Remember that you call on me today.
931Be near me, that I may remember you.

TREBONIUS

932Caesar I will,
933[aside] and so near will I be,aparte
934That your best friends shall wish I had been further.

CAESAR

935Good friends, go in, and taste some wine with me.
936And we, like friends, will straight way go together.

BRUTUS

937[aside] That every like is not the same, O Caesar,aparte
938The heart of Brutus yearns to think upon.

Exeunt

[2.3]

Enter Artemidorus [reading a paper].

ARTEMIDORUS

"Caesar, beware of Brutus; take heed of Cassius; come not near Casca; have an eye to Cinna; trust not Trebonius; mark well Metellus Cimber. Decius Brutus loves thee not. Thou hast wronged Caius Ligarius. There is but one mind in all these men, and it is bent against Caesar. If thou be'est not immortal, look about you. Security gives way to conspiracy. The mighty gods defend thee. Thy lover, Artemidorus."

939Here will I stand, till Caesar pass along,
940And as a suitor will I give him this.
941My heart laments that virtue cannot live
942Out of the teeth of emulation.
943If thou read this, O Caesar, thou mayst live;
944If not, the Fates with traitors do contrive.

Exit.

[2.4]

Enter Portia and Lucius.

PORTIA

945I prithee, boy, run to the Senate House.
946Stay not to answer me, but get thee gone.
947Why dost thou stay?

LUCIUS

Why dost thou stay? To know my errand, madam.

PORTIA

948I would have had thee there and here again
949Ere I can tell thee what thou shouldst do there.
950O constancy, be strong upon my side!
951Set a huge mountain 'tween my heart and tongue.
952I have a man's mind, but a woman's might.
953How hard it is for women to keep counsel.
954Art thou here yet?

LUCIUS

Art thou here yet? Madam, what should I do?
955Run to the Capitol, and nothing else?
956And so return to you, and nothing else?

PORTIA

957Yes, bring me word, boy, if thy lord look well,
958For he went sickly forth, and take good note
959What Caesar doth, what suitors press to him.
960Hark, boy, what noise is that?

LUCIUS

961I hear none, madam.

PORTIA

I hear none, madam. Prithee, listen well:
962I heard a bustling rumor, like a fray,
963And the wind brings it from the Capitol.

LUCIUS

964Sooth, madam, I hear nothing.

Enter the Soothsayer.

PORTIA

965Come hither, fellow. Which way hast thou been?

SOOTHSAYER

966At mine own house, good lady.

PORTIA

967What is't o'clock?

SOOTHSAYER

What is't o'clock? About the ninth hour, lady.

PORTIA

968Is Caesar yet gone to the Capitol?

SOOTHSAYER

969Madam, not yet. I go to take my stand
970To see him pass on to the Capitol.

PORTIA

971Thou hast some suit to Caesar, hast thou not?

SOOTHSAYER

972That I have, lady, if it will please Caesar
973To be so good to Caesar as to hear me:
974I shall beseech him to befriend himself.

PORTIA

975Why, know'st thou any harm's intended towards him?

SOOTHSAYER

976None that I know will be; much that I fear may chance.
977Good morrow to you. Here the street is narrow.
978The throng that follows Caesar at the heels
979Of senators, of praetors, common suitors,
980Will crowd a feeble man almost to death.
981I'll get me to a place more void, and there
982Speak to great Caesar as he comes along.

Exit

PORTIA

983I must go in. Ay me! How weak a thing
984The heart of woman is! O Brutus,
985The Heavens speed thee in thine enterprise.
986Sure the boy heard me. Brutus hath a suit
987That Caesar will not grant. O, I grow faint!
988Run Lucius, and commend me to my lord!
989Say I am merry. Come to me again,
990And bring me word what he doth say to thee.

Exeunt

Act III

[3.1]

Flourish.

Enter Caesar, Brutus, Cassius, Casca, Decius, Metellus, Trebonius, Cinna, Antony, Lepidus, Artemidorus, Publius, [Popilius Lena,] and the Soothsayer.

CAESAR

991The ides of March are come.

SOOTHSAYER

992Ay, Caesar, but not gone.

ARTEMIDORUS

993Hail, Caesar! Read this schedule.

DECIUS

994Trebonius doth desire you to o'erread,
995At your best leisure, this his humble suit.

ARTEMIDORUS

996O Caesar, read mine first, for mine's a suit
997That touches Caesar nearer. Read it, great Caesar.

CAESAR

998What touches us ourself shall be last served.

ARTEMIDORUS

999Delay not Caesar, read it instantly!

CAESAR

1000What, is the fellow mad?

PUBLIUS

What, is the fellow mad? Sirrah, give place.

CASSIUS

1001What, urge you your petitions in the street?
1002Come to the Capitol.

[Caesar and his train move away.]

POPILIUS

1003I wish your enterprise today may thrive.

CASSIUS

1004What enterprise, Popilius?

POPILIUS

What enterprise, Popilius? Fare you well.

[moves toward Caesar]

BRUTUS

1005What said Popilius Lena?

CASSIUS

1006He wished today our enterprise might thrive:
1007I fear our purpose is discoverèd.

BRUTUS

1008Look how he makes to Caesar: mark him.

CASSIUS

1009Casca, be sudden, for we fear prevention.
1010Brutus, what shall be done? If this be known,
1011Cassius or Caesar never shall turn back,
1012For I will slay myself.

BRUTUS

For I will slay myself. Cassius, be constant.
1013Popilius Lena speaks not of our purposes,
1014For look he smiles, and Caesar doth not change.

CASSIUS

1015Trebonius knows his time, for look you, Brutus,
1016He draws Mark Antony out of the way.

[Exeunt Antony and Trebonius.]

DECIUS

1017Where is Metellus Cimber? Let him go,
1018And presently prefer his suit to Caesar.

BRUTUS

1019He is addressed. Press near, and second him.

CINNA

1020Casca, you are the first that rears your hand.

CAESAR

1021Are we all ready? What is now amiss,
1022That Caesar and his senate must redress?

METELLUS

1023Most high, most mighty, and most puissant Caesar!
1024Metellus Cimber throws before thy seat
1025An humble heart.

CAESAR

An humble heart. I must prevent thee, Cimber.
1026These couchings and these lowly courtesies
1027Might fire the blood of ordinary men,
1028And turn preordinance and first decree
1029Into the lane of children. Be not fond
1030To think that Caesar bears such rebel blood
1031That will be thawed from the true quality
1032With that which melteth fools—I mean sweet words,
1033Low-crookèd curtsies, and base spaniel fawning.
1034Thy brother by decree is banishèd.
1035If thou doest bend, and pray, and fawn for him,
1036I spurn thee like a cur out of my way!
1037Know, Caesar doth not wrong, nor without cause
1038Will he be satisfied.

METELLUS

1039Is there no voice more worthy than my own
1040To sound more sweetly in great Caesar's ear
1041For the repealing of my banished brother?

BRUTUS

1042I kiss thy hand, but not in flattery, Caesar,
1043Desiring thee, that Publius Cimber may
1044Have an immediate freedom of repeal.

CAESAR

1045What, Brutus?

CASSIUS

What, Brutus? Pardon, Caesar! Caesar, pardon!
1046As low as to thy foot doth Cassius fall
1047To beg enfranchisement for Publius Cimber.

CAESAR

1048I could be well moved, if I were as you.
1049If I could pray to move, prayers would move me.
1050But I am constant as the northern star,
1051Of whose true fixed and resting quality,
1052There is no fellow in the firmament.
1053The skies are painted with unnumbered sparks;
1054They are all fire, and every one doth shine;
1055But there's but one in all doth hold his place.
1056So in the world: 'tis furnished well with men,
1057And men are flesh and blood, and apprehensive;
1058Yet in the number I do know but one
1059That unassailable holds on his rank
1060Unshaked of motion, and that I am he,
1061Let me a little show it, even in this:
1062That I was constant Cimber should be banished,
1063And constant do remain to keep him so.

CINNA

1064O Caesar—

CAESAR

O Caesar— Hence! Wilt thou lift up Olympus?

DECIUS

1065Great Caesar—

CAESAR

Great Caesar— Doth not Brutus bootless kneel?

CASCA

1066Speak, hands, for me!

They stab Caesar.

CAESAR

1067Et tu Brutè? —Then fall, Caesar!

Dies

CINNA

1068Liberty! Freedom! Tyranny is dead!
1069Run hence! Proclaim! Cry it about the streets!

CASSIUS

1070Some to the common pulpits and cry out!
1071Liberty, freedom, and enfranchisement!

BRUTUS

1072People and senators, be not affrighted.
1073Fly not! Stand still! Ambition's debt is paid.

CASCA

1074Go to the pulpit, Brutus.

DECIUS

Go to the pulpit, Brutus. And Cassius too.

BRUTUS

1075Where's Publius?

CINNA

1076Here, quite confounded with this mutiny.

METELLUS

1077Stand fast together, lest some friend of Caesar's
1078Should chance—

BRUTUS

1079Talk not of standing. Publius, good cheer.
1080There is no harm intended to your person,
1081Nor to no Roman else. So tell them, Publius.

CASSIUS

1082And leave us, Publius, lest that the people
1083Rushing on us, should do your age some mischief.

BRUTUS

1084Do so, and let no man abide this deed
1085But we the doers.

Enter Trebonius.

CASSIUS

1086Where is Antony?

TREBONIUS

Where is Antony? Fled to his house amazed.
1087Men, wives, and children, stare, cry out, and run
1088As it were doomsday.

BRUTUS

As it were doomsday. Fates, we will know your pleasures.
1089That we shall die, we know. 'Tis but the time
1090And drawing days out that men stand upon.

CASCA

1091Why, he that cuts off twenty years of life
1092Cuts off so many years of fearing death.

BRUTUS

1093Grant that, and then is death a benefit.
1094So are we Caesar's friends, that have abridged
1095His time of fearing death. Stoop, Romans, stoop,
1096And let us bathe our hands in Caesars blood
1097Up to the elbows, and besmear our swords.
1098Then walk we forth, even to the marketplace,
1099And waving our red weapons o'er our heads,
1100Let's all cry, "Peace, freedom, and liberty!"

CASSIUS

1101Stoop then, and wash. How many ages hence
1102Shall this our lofty scene be acted o'er,
1103In states unborn and accents yet unknown!

BRUTUS

1104How many times shall Caesar bleed in sport,
1105That now on Pompey's basis lies along,
1106No worthier than the dust!

CASSIUS

No worthier than the dust! So oft as that shall be,
1107So often shall the knot of us be called
1108The men that gave their country liberty.

DECIUS

1109What, shall we forth?

CASSIUS

What, shall we forth? Ay, every man away.
1110Brutus shall lead, and we will grace his heels
1111With the most boldest and best hearts of Rome.

Enter a servant.

BRUTUS

1112Soft, who comes here? A friend of Antony's.

SERVANT

1113Thus, Brutus, did my master bid me kneel.
1114Thus did Mark Antony bid me fall down,
1115And being prostrate, thus he bade me say:
1116"Brutus is noble, wise, valiant, and honest;
1117Caesar was mighty, bold, royal, and loving.
1118Say I love Brutus, and I honor him;
1119Say I feared Caesar, honored him, and loved him.
1120If Brutus will vouchsafe that Antony
1121May safely come to him, and be resolved
1122How Caesar hath deserved to lie in death,
1123Mark Antony shall not love Caesar dead
1124So well as Brutus living, but will follow
1125The fortunes and affairs of noble Brutus
1126Thorough the hazards of this untrod state,
1127With all true faith."
1128So says my master Antony.

BRUTUS

1129Thy master is a wise and valiant Roman;
1130I never thought him worse.
1131Tell him, so please him come unto this place,
1132He shall be satisfied, and by my honor
1133Depart untouched.

SERVANT

Depart untouched. I'll fetch him presently.

Exit servant.

BRUTUS

1134I know that we shall have him well to friend.

CASSIUS

1135I wish we may, but yet have I a mind
1136That fears him much, and my misgiving still
1137Falls shrewdly to the purpose.

Enter Antony.

BRUTUS

1138But here comes Antony: Welcome, Mark Antony.

ANTONY

1139O mighty Caesar! Dost thou lie so low?
1140Are all thy conquests, glories, triumphs, spoils,
1141Shrunk to this little measure? Fare thee well.
1142I know not, gentlemen, what you intend,
1143Who else must be let blood, who else is rank;
1144If I myself, there is no hour so fit
1145As Caesar's death's hour, nor no instrument
1146Of half that worth as those your swords, made rich
1147With the most noble blood of all this world.
1148I do beseech ye, if you bear me hard,
1149Now, whilst your purpled hands do reek and smoke,
1150Fulfill your pleasure. Live a thousand years,
1151I shall not find myself so apt to die;
1152No place will please me so, no mean of death,
1153As here by Caesar, and by you cut off,
1154The choice and master spirits of this age.

BRUTUS

1155O Antony! Beg not your death of us.
1156Though now we must appear bloody and cruel,
1157As by our hands and this our present act
1158You see we do. Yet see you but our hands
1159And this, the bleeding business they have done.
1160Our hearts you see not; they are pitiful,
1161And pity to the general wrong of Rome—
1162As fire drives out fire, so pity, pity—
1163Hath done this deed on Caesar. For your part,
1164To you our swords have leaden points, Mark Antony.
1165Our arms in strength of malice, and our hearts
1166Of brothers' temper, do receive you in,
1167With all kind love, good thoughts, and reverence.

CASSIUS

1168Your voice shall be as strong as any man's,
1169In the disposing of new dignities.

BRUTUS

1170Only be patient, till we have appeased
1171The multitude, beside themselves with fear,
1172And then we will deliver you the cause
1173Why I, that did love Caesar when I struck him,
1174Have thus proceeded.

ANTONY

Have thus proceeded. I doubt not of your wisdom.
1175Let each man render me his bloody hand.
1176First, Marcus Brutus. will I shake with you;
1177Next, Caius Cassius, do I take your hand;
1178Now, Decius Brutus yours; now yours, Metellus;
1179Yours, Cinna; and my valiant Casca, yours;
1180Though last, not least in love, yours, good Trebonius.
1181Gentlemen all. Alas, what shall I say?
1182My credit now stands on such slippery ground,
1183That one of two bad ways you must conceit me:
1184Either a coward, or a flatterer.
1185That I did love thee, Caesar, oh, 'tis true!
1186If then thy spirit look upon us now,
1187Shall it not grieve thee dearer than thy death
1188To see thy Antony making his peace,
1189Shaking the bloody fingers of thy foes—
1190Most noble — in the presence of thy corpse?
1191Had I as many eyes as thou hast wounds,
1192Weeping as fast as they stream forth thy blood,
1193It would become me better, than to close
1194In terms of friendship with thine enemies.
1195Pardon me Julius! Here was't thou bayed, brave hart,
1196Here did'st thou fall, and here thy hunters stand
1197Signed in thy spoil and crimsoned in thy Lethe.
1198O world! Thou wast the forest to this hart,
1199And this indeed, O world, the heart of thee!
1200How like a deer, strucken by many princes,
1201Dost thou here lie!

CASSIUS

1202Mark Antony—

ANTONY

Mark Antony— Pardon me, Caius Cassius.
1203The enemies of Caesar shall say this;
1204Then in a friend it is cold modesty.

CASSIUS

1205I blame you not for praising Caesar so,
1206But what compact mean you to have with us?
1207Will you be pricked in number of our friends,
1208Or shall we on, and not depend on you?

ANTONY

1209Therefore I took your hands, but was indeed
1210Swayed from the point by looking down on Caesar.
1211Friends am I with you all, and love you all,
1212Upon this hope, that you shall give me reasons,
1213Why and wherein Caesar was dangerous.

BRUTUS

1214Or else were this a savage spectacle.
1215Our reasons are so full of good regard
1216That were you, Antony, the son of Caesar,
1217You should be satisfied.

ANTONY

You should be satisfied. That's all I seek,
1218And am moreover suitor that I may
1219Produce his body to the marketplace,
1220And in the pulpit, as becomes a friend,
1221Speak in the order of his funeral.

BRUTUS

1222You shall, Mark Antony.

CASSIUS

You shall, Mark Antony. Brutus, a word with you:
1223You know not what you do! Do not consent
1224That Antony speak in his funeral.
1225Know you how much the people may be moved
1226By that which he will utter?

BRUTUS

By that which he will utter? By your pardon:
1227I will myself into the pulpit first,
1228And show the reason of our Caesar's death.
1229What Antony shall speak, I will protest
1230He speaks by leave and by permission,
1231And that we are contented Caesar shall
1232Have all true rites and lawful ceremonies.
1233It shall advantage more than do us wrong.

CASSIUS

1234I know not what may fall. I like it not.

BRUTUS

1235Mark Antony, here, take you Caesar's body.
1236You shall not in your funeral speech blame us,
1237But speak all good you can devise of Caesar,
1238And say you do't by our permission.
1239Else shall you not have any hand at all
1240About his funeral. And you shall speak
1241In the same pulpit whereto I am going,
1242After my speech is ended.

ANTONY

After my speech is ended. Be it so.
1243I do desire no more.

BRUTUS

1244Prepare the body, then, and follow us.

[Exeunt all but] Antony.

ANTONY

1245O pardon me, thou bleeding piece of earth,
1246That I am meek and gentle with these butchers!
1247Thou art the ruins of the noblest man
1248That ever livèd in the tide of times.
1249Woe to the hand that shed this costly blood!
1250Over thy wounds now do I prophesy,
1251Which like dumb mouths do ope their ruby lips,
1252To beg the voice and utterance of my tongue:
1253A curse shall light upon the limbs of men,
1254Domestic fury and fierce civil strife
1255Shall cumber all the parts of Italy;
1256Blood and destruction shall be so in use,
1257And dreadful objects so familiar,
1258That mothers shall but smile when they behold
1259Their infants quartered with the hands of war,
1260All pity choked with custom of fell deeds;
1261And Caesar's spirit ranging for revenge,
1262With Atè by his side come hot from hell,
1263Shall in these confines, with a monarch's voice,
1264Cry havoc and let slip the dogs of war,
1265That this foul deed shall smell above the earth
1266With carrion men, groaning for burial.

Enter Octavius' servant.

1267You serve Octavius Caesar, do you not?

SERVANT

1268I do, Mark Antony.

ANTONY

1269Caesar did write for him to come to Rome.

SERVANT

1270He did receive his letters and is coming,
1271And bid me say to you by word of mouth—
1272O Caesar!

ANTONY

1273Thy heart is big. Get thee apart and weep.
1274Passion I see is catching, for mine eyes,
1275Seeing those beads of sorrow stand in thine,
1276Begin to water. Is thy master coming?

SERVANT

1277He lies tonight within seven leagues of Rome.

ANTONY

1278Post back with speed, And tell him what hath chanced.
1279Here is a mourning Rome, a dangerous Rome,
1280No Rome of safety for Octavius yet.
1281Hie hence, and tell him so. Yet stay awhile;
1282Thou shalt not back till I have borne this corpse
1283Into the marketplace. There shall I try
1284In my oration how the people take
1285The cruel issue of these bloody men,
1286According to the which, thou shalt discourse
1287To young Octavius of the state of things.
1288Lend me your hand.

Exeunt

[3.2]

Enter Brutus and goes into the pulpit, and Cassius with the plebians.

PLEBIANS

1289We will be satisfied! Let us be satisfied!

BRUTUS

1290Then follow me, and give me audience, friends.
1291Cassius, go you into the other street,
1292And part the numbers.
1293Those that will hear me speak, let 'em stay here;
1294Those that will follow Cassius, go with him;
1295And public reasons shall be rendered
1296Of Caesar's death.

1 PLEBIAN

Of Caesar's death. I will hear Brutus speak.

2 PLEBIAN

1297I will hear Cassius and compare their reasons,
1298When severally we hear them renderèd.

[Exit Cassius, with some of the Plebians.]

3 PLEBIAN

1299The noble Brutus is ascended. Silence!

BRUTUS

1300Be patient till the last.

Romans, countrymen, and lovers! Hear me for my cause, and be silent, that you may hear. Believe me for mine honor, and have respect to mine honor that you may believe. Censure me in your wisdom, and awake your senses that you may the better judge. If there be any in this assembly, any dear friend of Caesar's, to him I say, that Brutus' love to Caesar was no less than his. If then that friend demand why Brutus rose against Caesar, this is my answer: not that I loved Caesar less, but that I loved Rome more. Had you rather Caesar were living and die all slaves, than that Caesar were dead to live all free men? As Caesar loved me, I weep for him; as he was fortunate, I rejoice at it; as he was valiant, I honor him; but as he was ambitious, I slew him. There is tears for his love; joy, for his fortune; honor, for his valor; and death, for his ambition. Who is here so base that would be a bondman? If any, speak, for him have I offended. Who is here so rude that would not be a Roman? If any, speak, for him have I offended. Who is here so vile that will not love his country? If any, speak, for him have I offended. I pause for a reply.

ALL

None, Brutus, none.

BRUTUS

Then none have I offended. I have done no more to Caesar than you shall do to Brutus. The question of his death is enrolled in the Capitol; his glory not extenuated, wherein he was worthy; nor his offenses enforced, for which he suffered death. Enter Mark Antony, with Caesar's body.
Here comes his body, mourned by Mark Antony, who, though he had no hand in his death, shall receive the benefit of his dying, a place in the commonwealth, as which of you shall not? With this, I depart, that as I slew my best lover for the good of Rome, I have the same dagger for myself, when it shall please my country to need my death.

ALL

Live Brutus! Live! Live!

1 PLEBIAN

1301Bring him with triumph home unto his house.

2 PLEBIAN

1302Give him a statue with his ancestors.

3 PLEBIAN

1303Let him be Caesar.

4 PLEBIAN

Let him be Caesar. Caesar's better parts,
1304Shall be crowned in Brutus.

1 PLEBIAN

1305We'll bring him to his house with shouts and clamors.

BRUTUS

1306My countrymen!

2 PLEBIAN

1307Peace! Silence! Brutus speaks.

1 PLEBIAN

1308Peace, ho!

BRUTUS

1309Good countrymen, let me depart alone,
1310And for my sake, stay here with Antony.
1311Do grace to Caesar's corpse, and grace his speech
1312Tending to Caesar's glories, which Mark Antony
1313By our permission is allowed to make.
1314I do entreat you, not a man depart,
1315Save I alone, till Antony have spoke.

Exit

1 PLEBIAN

1316Stay, ho! And let us hear Mark Antony.

3 PLEBIAN

1317Let him go up into the public chair.
1318We'll hear him. Noble Antony, go up.

ANTONY

1319For Brutus' sake, I am beholding to you.

4 PLEBIAN

1320What does he say of Brutus?

3 PLEBIAN

1321He says, for Brutus' sake
1322He finds himself beholding to us all.

4 PLEBIAN

1323'Twere best he speak no harm of Brutus here!

1 PLEBIAN

1324This Caesar was a tyrant.

3 PLEBIAN

This Caesar was a tyrant. Nay, that's certain:
1325We are blest that Rome is rid of him.

2 PLEBIAN

1326Peace! Let us hear what Antony can say.

ANTONY

1327You gentle Romans—

ALL

You gentle Romans— Peace, ho! Let us hear him.

ANTONY

1328Friends! Romans! Countrymen! Lend me your ears.
1329I come to bury Caesar, not to praise him.
1330The evil that men do lives after them;
1331The good is oft interrèd with their bones:
1332So let it be with Caesar. The noble Brutus
1333Hath told you Caesar was ambitious.
1334If it were so, it was a grievous fault,
1335And grievously hath Caesar answered it.
1336Here, under leave of Brutus and the rest—
1337For Brutus is an honorable man,
1338So are they all, all honorable men—
1339Come I to speak in Caesar's funeral.
1340He was my friend: faithful and just to me.
1341But Brutus says he was ambitious,
1342And Brutus is an honorable man.
1343He hath brought many captives home to Rome,
1344Whose ransoms did the general coffers fill.
1345Did this in Caesar seem ambitious?
1346When that the poor have cried, Caesar hath wept;
1347Ambition should be made of sterner stuff.
1348Yet Brutus says he was ambitious,
1349And Brutus is an honorable man.
1350You all did see that on the Lupercal
1351I thrice presented him a kingly crown,
1352Which he did thrice refuse. Was this ambition?
1353Yet Brutus says he was ambitious,
1354And sure he is an honorable man.
1355I speak not to disprove what Brutus spoke,
1356But here I am, to speak what I do know.
1357You all did love him once, not without cause.
1358What cause withholds you, then, to mourn for him?
1359O judgment! Thou art fled to brutish beasts,
1360And men have lost their reason! Bear with me;
1361My heart is in the coffin there with Caesar,
1362And I must pause till it come back to me.

1 PLEBIAN

1363Methinks there is much reason in his sayings.

2 PLEBIAN

1364If thou consider rightly of the matter,
1365Caesar has had great wrong.

3 PLEBIAN

Caesar has had great wrong. Has he, masters?
1366I fear there will a worse come in his place.

4 PLEBIAN

1367Marked ye his words? He would not take the crown;
1368Therefore 'tis certain he was not ambitious.

1 PLEBIAN

1369If it be found so, some will dear abide it.

2 PLEBIAN

1370Poor soul, his eyes are red as fire with weeping.

3 PLEBIAN

1371There's not a nobler man in Rome than Antony.

4 PLEBIAN

1372Now mark him, he begins again to speak.

ANTONY

1373But yesterday, the word of Caesar might
1374Have stood against the world. Now lies he there,
1375And none so poor to do him reverence.
1376O masters! If I were disposed to stir
1377Your hearts and minds to mutiny and rage,
1378I should do Brutus wrong, and Cassius wrong,
1379Who you all know are honorable men.
1380I will not do them wrong; I rather choose
1381To wrong the dead, to wrong myself and you,
1382Than I will wrong such honorable men.
1383But here's a parchment with the seal of Caesar;
1384I found it in his closet. 'Tis his will.
1385Let but the commons hear this testament,
1386Which pardon me, I do not mean to read,
1387And they would go and kiss dead Caesar's wounds,
1388And dip their napkins in his sacred blood,
1389Yea, beg a hair of him for memory,
1390And dying, mention it within their wills,
1391Bequeathing it as a rich legacy
1392Unto their issue.

4 PLEBIAN

1393We'll hear the will! Read it, Mark Antony!

ALL

1394The will! The will! We will hear Caesar's will!

ANTONY

1395Have patience, gentle friends. I must not read it.
1396It is not meet you know how Caesar loved you.
1397You are not wood, you are not stones, but men;
1398And being men, hearing the will of Caesar,
1399It will inflame you; it will make you mad.
1400'Tis good you know not that you are his heirs,
1401For if you should, oh, what would come of it?

4 PLEBIAN

1402Read the will! We'll hear it, Antony!
1403You shall read us the will! Caesar's will!

ANTONY

1404Will you be patient? Will you stay awhile?
1405I have o'ershot myself to tell you of it,
1406I fear I wrong the honorable men
1407Whose daggers have stabbed Caesar. I do fear it.

4 PLEBIAN

1408They were traitors! "Honorable men"?

ALL

1409The will! The testament!

2 PLEBIAN

1410They were villains, murderers! The will! Read the will!

ANTONY

1411You will compel me then to read the will?
1412Then make a ring about the corpse of Caesar,
1413And let me show you him that made the will.
1414Shall I descend? And will you give me leave?

ALL

1415Come down!

2 PLEBIAN

Come down! Descend!

4 PLEBIAN

1416A ring!
A ring! Stand round!

1 PLEBIAN

1417Stand from the hearse! Stand from the body!

2 PLEBIAN

1418Room for Antony! Most noble Antony!

ANTONY

1419Nay, press not so upon me! Stand far'er off.

ALL

1420Stand back! Room! Bear back!

ANTONY

1421If you have tears, prepare to shed them now.
1422You all do know this mantle; I remember
1423The first time ever Caesar put it on.
1424'Twas on a summer's evening in his tent,
1425That day he overcame the Nervii.
1426Look, in this place ran Cassius' dagger through.
1427See what a rent the envious Casca made.
1428Through this, the well-belovèd Brutus stabbed,
1429And as he plucked his cursèd steel away,
1430Mark how the blood of Caesar followed it,
1431As rushing out of doors to be resolved
1432If Brutus so unkindly knocked or no;
1433For Brutus, as you know, was Caesar's angel.
1434Judge, O you gods, how dearly Caesar loved him.
1435This was the most unkindest cut of all,
1436For when the noble Caesar saw him stab,
1437Ingratitude, more strong than traitors' arms,
1438Quite vanquished him; then burst his mighty heart,
1439And in his mantle muffling up his face,
1440Even at the base of Pompey's statue,
1441Which all the while ran blood, great Caesar fell.
1442Oh, what a fall was there, my countrymen!
1443Then I, and you, and all of us fell down,
1444Whilst bloody treason flourished over us.
1445Oh, now you weep, and I perceive you feel
1446The dint of pity. These are gracious drops.
1447Kind souls, what weep you when you but behold
1448Our Caesar's vesture wounded? Look you here!
1449Here is himself, marred as you see with traitors.

1 PLEBIAN

1450Oh, piteous spectacle!

2 PLEBIAN

Oh, piteous spectacle! O noble Caesar!

3 PLEBIAN

1451Oh, woeful day!

4 PLEBIAN

Oh, woeful day! Oh, traitors! Villains!

1 PLEBIAN

1452Oh, most bloody sight!

2 PLEBIAN

Oh, most bloody sight! We will be revenged!

ALL

1453Revenge! About! Seek! Burn! Fire! Kill! Slay!
1454Let not a traitor live!

ANTONY

Let not a traitor live! Stay, countrymen!

1 PLEBIAN

1455Peace there! Hear the noble Antony!

2 PLEBIAN

1456We'll hear him! We'll follow him! We'll die with him.

ANTONY

1457Good friends! Sweet friends! Let me not stir you up
1458To such a sudden flood of mutiny.
1459They that have done this deed are honorable.
1460What private griefs they have, alas, I know not,
1461That made them do it. They are wise and honorable,
1462And will no doubt with reasons answer you.
1463I come not, friends, to steal away your hearts.
1464I am no orator, as Brutus is,
1465But, as you know me all, a plain blunt man
1466That love my friend, and that they know full well
1467That gave me public leave to speak of him:
1468For I have neither wit, nor words, nor worth,
1469Action, nor utterance, nor the power of speech
1470To stir men's blood. I only speak right on.
1471I tell you that which you yourselves do know;
1472Show you sweet Caesar's wounds, poor poor dumb mouths,
1473And bid them speak for me. But were I Brutus,
1474And Brutus Antony, there were an Antony
1475Would ruffle up your spirits and put a tongue
1476In every wound of Caesar that should move
1477The stones of Rome to rise and mutiny.

ALL

1478We'll mutiny!

1 PLEBIAN

We'll mutiny! We'll burn the house of Brutus!

3 PLEBIAN

1479Away then! Come, seek the conspirators!

ANTONY

1480Yet hear me countrymen! Yet hear me speak!

ALL

1481Peace, ho! Hear Antony! Most noble Antony!

ANTONY

1482Why, friends, you go to do you know not what.
1483Wherein hath Caesar thus deserved your loves?
1484Alas you know not. I must tell you then:
1485You have forgot the will I told you of.

ALL

1486Most true! The will! Let's stay and hear the will!

ANTONY
